# M2: every computed p, checked against the model

*Written 2026-11-07 by M2 (brief `briefs/M2-check-computed-p.md`), on branch `claude/project-thread-pbr8zl`. What was checked: every p in `data/president/` and `data/congress/` as they stand on `main` (D1's #23 and D2's #24; unchanged by the later merges). The standard is `model/MODEL.md` and `model/MATH.md`. The levels D3 brings (`data/state-upper/`, `data/state-lower/`, `data/local/`, `data/measure/`) are checked in the second pass below (§5–9), with Congress and the governors again after D2's #38, #51 and #53, and D3's levels again after his #59. The third pass (§10) checks D2's #67 and #74 and D3's #60–#73 and #75 as they landed on 2026-11-07; the fourth (§11) D3's #82, #84 and #85; the fifth (§12) D3's #86, #87 and #88, with a2a4b0a as the fixed end; the sixth (§13) D3's #90, with b9e0702 as the fixed end. Nothing outside `checks/` is changed; no data and no pipeline.*

**Reproduce.** `sh checks/m2/run_all.sh` from the repository root writes `checks/m2/OUTPUT.txt` (about 45 minutes once the raw files are summarized: c1–c7 on today's files, c17, the second pass's c8–c15, the third pass's c16 and c18–c30, the fourth pass's c31–c33, the fifth pass's c34–c36, the sixth pass's c37). §1–4 cite the lines of the first pass's OUTPUT.txt, the run of record of c1–c7 against the files of #23 and #24, which the history holds at d11cae2 (`git show d11cae2:checks/m2/OUTPUT.txt`); since the third pass c1–c7 run again on today's files (what they read was changed, never what they test) and `c17_first_pass_diff.py` prints every line that differs from d11cae2's with its cause (§10.2). Every number in this report is on the OUTPUT.txt line it cites (**L** = line of OUTPUT.txt). The scripts in `checks/m2/` import nothing from `pipeline/` or `model/`; `m2lib.py` is their own implementation of the model's formulas. The sources they read are fetched outside the repository (`m2lib.CACHE`: the Economist's `economist_model_output.zip`, 538's four checking-our-work CSVs, the Economist's `us-potus-model` README) and their sha256 is printed and matched to the record on each run.

**Marks.** [measured] a number this run computes from the record and the sources; [stated] a source's or a report's own words; [inferred] my reading of measured things.

## Summary

1. **Every p is what the model says it is**, from its contest's own recorded inputs, at every level. The presidency reproduces to 3 × 10⁻¹¹ (the model's own forecast) and exactly (538, the Economist). Congress reproduces within the bound set by the files' six-decimal rounding of mean and sd: 0 of 11,876 p blocks beyond it. No p is off by a factor of two; none is off at all beyond that rounding.
2. **The second stage holds** by methods unrelated to the pipeline's: the Electoral College's W to 3 × 10⁻¹² in every year 1984–2024 (Maine's and Nebraska's districts included), the chambers' P(decides | tie) to 10⁻¹² (Senate) and 3 × 10⁻⁷ (House), and Monte Carlo within 3 standard errors everywhere.
3. **The forecasts are honest**, with four things to weigh: (a) D2's diagnosis of the governors ("too wide in the middle, so p too high") is not supported; the forecast is slightly too wide everywhere, which makes p slightly too *low*, and the 0-of-4.4 is specific to the 5,000-vote line. (b) Leave-one-cycle-out versus a fit on earlier cycles only moves the sum of p by up to 19% in the early cycles (House 2014) and not at all in 2024. (c) The presidency's 2024 horizon is matched to within 3 days, as closely as the sources allow. (d) 538's 20% rule guards the sd's error, not p's; p's error is |z² − 1| times the sd's, and 174 of the 1,320 538-first entries exceed 20% on p.
4. **The published gaps are explained** as far as the sources allow: the Economist's far-state gaps come from a different estimator of the same draws (a fitted tail where the window holds a dozen draws) and its level from a different n, while a ± 20–40% scatter in WI, NH, MN, NV and AZ remains that neither estimator reproduces from these draws; GKB's 1992 figure rests on an earlier, wider forecast, not a different method; GSE's 2008 figures rest on poll-based state means and narrower state errors.
5. **The presidential record matches the forecasts** at every margin from 1 vote to 100,000.
6. **Every first-screen figure reproduces by hand.**

## 2. Reasoning

### 2.1 Item 1: every p from its own inputs

**Method.** `c1_recompute.py` reads each contest's `p` block, finds the forecast its `under` names among the contest's own `forecasts`, and recomputes f(½) and p = f(½)/(2n) with `m2lib`'s own normal and t4 densities (t4 at scale sd/√2) and, for the model's own presidential forecast, its own convolution of the normal national error with the t4 state error by quadrature (`own_pres_pdf`). The Economist's p is recounted from the raw draws (`c2`). n is the contest's `n_other_voters`.

**Tolerances, and why.** Presidency, the model's own: 10⁻⁴, because D1 integrates on a 401-point grid and I by adaptive quadrature; the two should agree far better than that, and do (2.6 × 10⁻¹¹). Closed forms (538, the Economist's counts, the event, the product): 10⁻⁹ or exact. Congress: the files record mean and sd to six decimals, while p was computed from the unrounded values, so the recorded mean and sd cannot give the recorded p exactly; the bound for each contest is the largest relative change in f(½) over the four corners mean ± 5 × 10⁻⁷, sd ± 5 × 10⁻⁷, plus 10⁻⁹. Every difference lies inside its bound (the largest at 0.999 of it), so the differences are the rounding and nothing else is visible beneath it. The loop is closed from the other side by `c4`, which rebuilds the model's own forecast from the contests' recorded inputs (lean, incumbency, midterm, president's party) and reproduces every governor mean and sd to 5.3 × 10⁻⁷, the rounding again [measured, L615].

*Two readings for the orchestrator.* The record's p is right; a reader recomputing p from the file's own mean and sd will differ from it by up to 7 × 10⁻⁴ relative (typically 10⁻⁵). Either leave it (p is the figure; mean and sd are shown rounded) or record mean and sd, or f(½), to more digits so that the file reproduces itself. I choose neither.

**The exact sum.** No contest is near the switch (1.7). For every Congress p the lattice sum of MATH §1.2(a), ½ P(X = n/2) with X | v ~ Binomial(n, v) integrated against the forecast over (0, 1), is within 0.4% of the density form where p ≥ 10⁻⁶ and within 1% where p ≥ 10⁻⁸. Beyond that the density form runs low by up to 6% at z = 8.8, where p is 10⁻²¹. The model's convolved form (MATH §1.3, variance sd² + 1/(4n)) reproduces the lattice sum to 0.11% everywhere, so the error is understood; what MATH §1.3 does not say is that its "2% once n sd² > 6.25" holds at mean ½ and the error grows with z. A line in MATH would close it; no p on the site is affected. (A forecast truncated to (0, 1) and renormalized, which the model does not do, would raise the far seats' p by 1/mass: 1.31 for 2018-house-NY-15 at mean 0.96; 1.000 for every p ≥ 10⁻⁶ except by 10⁻⁴.)

### 2.2 Item 2: the second stage

**The College.** D1's W is a 401-point grid over the national swing with the other units' electoral votes counted by convolution of their win chances. `c2_college.py` counts them instead by a Fourier (characteristic-function) product on the unit circle with prefix and suffix products, so that each unit's "others" distribution comes from one pass, on a 1,201-point Simpson grid over ± 12 s_d; and, unrelated to both, by importance-sampling Monte Carlo (10⁶ draws of the national swing and the state errors with the unit held at the tie, the window weight averaged). Fourier agrees with the record to 3 × 10⁻¹², which is what two convergent quadratures of a smooth integrand should give; Monte Carlo agrees within its standard error for all 573 unit-years (largest |z| 2.47; about 0.1 beyond 3 se expected by chance, 0 seen). The districts of 2024 (ME-1, ME-2, NE-1, NE-2, NE-3) are in the count with their own electoral vote and sd. Under 538's marginals the same, with the national swing's sd from the model's own year, which is what the record's method text says and what the code does [measured]. The Economist's 204 window counts are recounted exactly from the raw file.

One finding (2.4): where the Economist's window holds a dozen weighted draws (OH 13, IA 12, CO 28), W depends on the window's width by factors of 3 to 10. The record marks a W as thin only under 10 weighted draws, so OH and IA are not marked. That is a labelling question, not an error: the record says what it counted.

**The chambers.** `c3_chambers.py` rebuilds each chamber-year's inputs from the files (every seat's mean, whether modelled, inferred or fixed; the seats not up; the pivots), and counts the other seats by the same Fourier product on a Simpson grid, with the swing given the tie as MATH §4.2 writes it, d | tie ~ N((½ − μ) s_a²/sd², s_a² s_e²/sd²). The pivots are read from the record (House 217 of the others; Senate 49 or 50 less the Democrats not up and the fixed seats, with the Vice President at ½ in presidential years, as the record now stands). Every Senate seat agrees to 10⁻¹², every House seat to 3 × 10⁻⁷ (the House's sums run over 400 seats and 10⁻⁶ was the tolerance set); the unconditional P(decides), recorded for comparison, to 4 × 10⁻¹⁴. Monte Carlo (2 × 10⁶ draws per Senate year, 4 × 10⁵ for the House of 2018, 2022 and 2024) agrees within its error for every seat. *Not checked:* the `P_decides_given_tie_if_VP_D` and `_if_VP_R` fields; when D2's Vice-President change lands, the presidential-year Senate seats' P(decides | tie) change and `c3` should be rerun on them.

**538 × own (2.6).** Under 538 the control p is 538's seat p times P(decides | tie), and P conditions the national swing on a tie at the **model's own** mean for the seat, with the other seats the model's own. Arithmetically this is exactly what the record says (1.10). As a model it holds two forecasts of the same seat at once: the chance of a tie is 538's, but what a tie *tells* the chamber is the model's. Where the two means agree it is harmless; where 538 puts a seat at 0.48 and the model at 0.33 (2018-house-WV-03), a tie is a small surprise to 538 and a large swing to the model, and P is 0.006 as recorded against 0.023 conditioned on 538's mean. Over all seats the medians are near one (table 2.6), the extremes are factors, and the sum of control p moves by −30% (House 2020, reading c) to +11% (House 2018, reading b). *Two readings.* (a) Keep it as recorded and say so in the method text (the record does): a published seat forecast through the model's chamber stage. (b) or (c) Condition on 538's mean, or take 538's mean and sd as the seat's marginal with s_e,i = √(sd₅₃₈² − s_a²), as the presidency already treats 538's marginals; this makes the seat and the chamber see one world, at the price of mixing 538's one-seat sd with the model's correlation. Reading (c) is the presidency's rule; it is the one that moves most.

### 2.3 Item 3: the forecasts

**The governors.** D2's arithmetic holds: under the record's p (the site's mix, 152 own and 66 538-first), 4.38 governor races were expected within 5,000 votes and none came, P 0.013. The explanation offered, "the forecast runs too wide in the middle so p is too high", fails on both counts. First, the forecast is not too wide in the middle in share: counting races by |share − ½| ≤ h gives expected/seen of 3.7/3, 7.5/6, 14.9/17, 29.7/34, 72.6/82 for h from a quarter point to five points, so the record has, if anything, slightly *more* near-even races than forecast at every width above half a point. Second, a forecast that is too wide has too little density at its centre, so a too-wide forecast gives p too *low*; narrowing it (A3) raises p by 4%. The forecast is about 5% too wide overall (z sd 0.95), not in the middle particularly (the band ratios are 0.96, 0.94, 0.95 for |mean − ½| up to 10 points and 0.86 beyond it). What is left is the vote count: 14 races finished within one point of even, and with 180,000 to a million voters each, none happened to land within 5,000 votes; the nearest are Connecticut 2010 (6,404), Montana 2012 (7,571), Rhode Island 2006 (7,803) and Minnesota 2010 (8,770). Given those 14 close races, 2.0 would be expected within 5,000 and the chance of none is 0.13. The line at 5,000 is the unlucky one: at 10,000 votes 4 were seen against 8.8 (P 0.064), at 20,000 13 against 17.5 (P 0.17). The 66 races where 538 stands first contribute the other part: 538's own forecasts for the governors of 2018 and 2022 ran wide (z sd 0.78) [measured], which lowers their p, not raises it.

*Options, with what each does to p (none applied, each leave-one-cycle-out like the record):* A1 t4 tails at the same sd: p × 0.997 in the median but 0.63 to 1.32 across races, and the 50% interval worse (0.54), so a worse forecast; A2 an error sd that varies with |mean − ½|: z sd 1.000 and p × 1.046 (0.96–1.12), the cleanest calibration; A3 the sd scaled by the back-test's 0.93: z sd 1.000, p × 1.039 (0.86–1.08), the simplest; A4 a lagged term, the state's last governor race, which D2's method lacks: on the 187 races of 2010–2024 that have one, b_lag = +0.25, s_e 0.0666 against 0.0702 without it on the same rows, log score 1.064 → 1.143, p × 1.030 (0.60–1.32), the most informative and the one that changes the method rather than its scale. Every option raises the expected count within 5,000 votes (4.1–4.2) because every one sharpens the forecast; none makes 0 of 4.4 likelier. I recommend nothing; A4 is the only one that adds information, and A2 or A3 the only ones that fix the calibration figure the report quotes.

**Leave one cycle out.** The record fits each cycle on the other nine, so a 2014 forecast uses 2016–2024's coefficients and national misses. `c4_congress.py` refits on earlier cycles only (an expanding window from 2014, the first year with four earlier cycles for the national swing's three terms), with the national sd both as the record has it and from the earlier cycles alone. The table at 3.4 gives the ratio of the sums of the model's own p. Early cycles move most (House 2014 × 0.81, 2016 × 0.86; Senate 2016 × 0.89; governor 2014 × 0.89), because the early fits have wider s_e (House 0.0568 against 0.0489) and the coefficients drift (b_lean 0.77 → 0.81); 2022 is within 3% and 2024 is the same forecast by construction, since leaving 2024 out *is* a fit on the earlier cycles. The site p affected are the model's-own ones: House 2014 (358), 2016 (343), 2024 (373, unchanged), Senate 2024 (32, unchanged), governor 2014 (33). *Two readings:* leave-one-out is the record's standard and uses what the data hold; earlier-only is what a forecaster then could have fitted, and gives p lower by up to a fifth in 2014–2016 and higher by up to 4% for governors in 2018–2022. The orchestrator marked this as the maker's to correct; the sizes are now known. (D2's own test of an expanding window and its finding that it "runs too wide in its early years" is confirmed: the same sums, lower.)

**The presidency's horizon and scale.** `c4b_president_538.py` retypes D1's fit from the record's averages and results: for each year, the national error sd is the root-mean-square miss of the earlier cycles' averages at the same horizon, and the state sd that of the uniform swing's state misses; every year reproduces the record's s_nat and s_state exactly (largest difference 0). For 2024 the input is 538's average of 12 September, the last reachable, and the errors are fitted to the 9 September averages of 1976–2020: a horizon 3 days longer than the input's, which is as close as the record's sources allow; the eve-fitted sd (0.0190) belongs to eve inputs that do not exist for 2024, and using it would raise Pennsylvania's f(½) by 18% while W moved too. The 12 September average missed the result by −0.84 national sd, an ordinary miss. The t4 scale is sd/√2 in both pipeline formulas, retyped and checked against scipy at 10⁻¹⁶, so the t4 has the stated sd; the other readings (scale = sd; a normal) would give 0.83 and 0.90 of the record's f(½).

**538's implied sd.** The run counts inferred from the chances' step are right for every model-year (3.7); for Senate 2016 the only rows off the 20,000 step are the "US" control-of-the-chamber rows, which are not seat chances, so the record's 20,000 stands; Senate 2014's chances have no step at all (20 decimals), and the record takes them as exact with no sampling error, which is right if 538's 2014 model was analytic and unknowable otherwise; the 34 entries stand first on that reading. The error formula reproduces to the record's rounding (3.8). The rule itself guards the sd's relative error, which is the quantity the 20% is stated on. But p = φ(z)/(sd × 2n) depends on sd through both the density and the scale, and its relative error is |z² − 1| times the sd's: at z = 1 p does not depend on sd at all; at z = 3.94 (2018 House lite entries at 538's rounding of 0.0 or 100.0%) a 7% sd error is a 105% p error. Over the 1,320 entries the site shows first, 174 exceed 20% on p (House 155, presidency 10, Senate 7, governor 2), 25 exceed 100% (22 House, 2 presidency, 1 Senate). The 190 entries the record rejects would all be rejected by a p rule too. *Two readings:* keep the sd rule, which is what the record states and is honest about the sd; or state the rule on p's error, which is what the reader sees, and the 174 entries would fall to the model's own forecast (which stands beside them in the files already). Either is defensible; the second changes 174 site figures.

### 2.4 Item 4: the published figures

**The Economist, 2020.** The Economist does not state its method for `p_decisive_vote` [stated, D1], and its public repository (157 files at commit b89e602) holds no code for it [measured]. Two things explain the spread. (i) *Which estimator.* The record counts ties in a ± 0.01 window of the share and weights by whether the unit decides: an empirical count. GSE's method (model §4; MATH §3.4) multiplies P(the state is necessary) by the density at zero of a t4 fitted to the vote margin among the necessary draws. `c5_published.py` runs GSE's method on the Economist's own draws: it gives 0.903 of the published figure in the median over all 51 states (0.35–1.60; within 25% in 30), the same ratio the record's window has over its 16, and it runs at 0.86–0.98 of the window's own count where the window holds hundreds of draws (PA 0.98, MI 0.86, FL 0.91, AZ 0.89, GA 0.89, NC 0.89, TX 0.90), the t4 fit's shape being a little flatter at zero than the draws. Where the window holds a dozen draws the two part: for Ohio the window counts 13 weighted draws (6.9 × 10⁻¹⁰), the density among the necessary draws is 6.7 × 10⁻¹⁰ by a local count, and a t4 *fitted* to those draws puts 1.76 × 10⁻⁹ at zero because the fit's tail is heavier than the draws' own; the published figure is 4.98 × 10⁻⁹. So Ohio's 0.14 is the gap between an empirical count and a fitted tail in a state 3.4 sd from necessity, where the draws cannot say which is right. (ii) *Which n.* At the level, both the window and GSE-t4 sit at about 0.90 of the published figure; if the Economist's method is GSE's, the n it implies is 0.82 of the 2020 count and 0.97 of the 2016 count in the median, which is consistent with the Economist having used 2016-sized turnout [inferred; the Economist does not say]. (iii) *What remains.* In Wisconsin, New Hampshire, Minnesota and Nevada both estimators sit at 0.62–0.68 (GSE-t4) and 0.67–0.92 (the window) of the published figure, and in Arizona at 1.18 and 1.32; these windows hold 135 to 600 weighted draws, so sampling error (4–9%) does not cover it, and no reading of these draws I have tried (window widths, a local-linear fit, GSE with normal or t4 tails at either scale) reproduces them. The published figure is then not a function of these 80,000 draws by either method, which points to another draw set or another n per state (turnout by state is the one input neither method here varies). *Likelier right:* for the site's definition — the chance of a tie by the draws' own count, with the certified n — the record's figure, whose counts are exact. The Economist's is a different estimator with an unstated n and, it seems, other draws; it is not wrong, it is a different question. *What would settle it:* the Economist's script for `p_decisive_vote`, or its per-state n and draw set.

**GKB 1992.** `c5` runs the record's 1992 College stage under other inputs. The record's forecast is the eve average (0.5467), s_nat 0.022, s_state 0.0425 (t4): it gives the Democrat 0.956 of the College, so the College is rarely close and the average voter's p is 1 in 45.6 million. GKB's errors (Table 1 of the paper, as the ledger B4 records them: national 2.5%, regional 2.5%, state 3.5%) on the same mean give 1 in 30.9 million; the 9 September average and its fit (s_nat 0.035) 1 in 29.5 million; and a mean nearer even (0.52, which is not in any source) 1 in 13 million. So the method is not the gap; the forecast is: GKB forecast earlier in the year, with wider errors and a closer race, and such a forecast gives about 1 in 30 million; "about 1 in 20 million" needs their state means too. *Likelier right:* each for its own inputs; the site's figure is the model's with the eve forecast, and that is what the record says it is. *What would settle it:* GKB's state means (their forecast table), which the record does not hold.

**GSE 2008.** GSE's forecast was Silver's of 24 October 2008 from state polls, with their own turnout; the record's is the uniform swing from the 3 November national average with s_state 0.037. The nearest poll-based 2008 forecast on hand is the Economist's 2008 back-test (its README's table, read as recorded): its means miss the results by 0.016 on average against the uniform swing's 0.027, and its state sds are about half the record's. That is the whole gap: in the close states a better-informed mean and a narrower error put more density at even, and in California a narrower error puts far less (the record's CA is 9 sd from even under s_state 0.037 and much further under 0.02). Through the record's own College stage, the own means with a poll-sized state sd give NM 1 in 12.9M, OH 12.6M and CA 970M, close to GSE's "about 1 in 10 million" and "about 1 in a billion"; the Economist's election-day means give the Democrat 1.000 of the College, so no national sd fits them and the close states come out *lower* (NM 65M). *Likelier right:* GSE's, for 2008 as a forecast made on 24 October from the state polls; the record's, for what the model says with the inputs it has (no polls, uniform swing). The site shows the model's and says so. *What would settle it:* Silver's 24 October state means and sds, which are not reachable (D1's unread list).

### 2.5 Item 5: the record against the forecasts (presidency)

`c6_record.py` does for the presidency what D2 did for Congress: under the forecast each p used, the expected number of contests decided by k votes or fewer, against the number seen. In the cdf form (the forecast's mass over the band, which is exact for the normal and t4 forecasts and for the convolution) the expected / seen are 0.002 / 0 at k = 1, 0.02 / 0 at 10, 0.20 / 0 at 100, 1.98 / 2 at 1,000, 9.9 / 7 at 5,000, 19.7 / 15 at 10,000, 39.3 / 34 at 20,000, 97.1 / 98 at 50,000 and 187.6 / 189 at 100,000; the Poisson chance of the count seen or fewer is at least 0.17 at every k, and of the count seen or more at least 0.47. The forecasts pass. The two within 1,000 votes are New Mexico 2000 (366) and Florida 2000 (537). Under each forecast separately (538's three 2016 models, polls-plus over 2016–2020, the Economist's 16, the model's own 566) the same picture. One note: D2's test uses the density form (expected = 2pk), which is right for k up to about 20,000 for the presidency (within 0.4%) and understates the expected count at 50,000 and 100,000 (89 and 149 against 97 and 188), because f varies across so wide a band; MATH §8.2 should say the cdf form for large k.

### 2.6 Item 6: the first screen

`c7_first_screen.py` recomputes by hand, from each contest's own mean, sd and n with its own density formulas, the average voter's p in every year and the closest three states, and the largest p of each Congress level and year, and sets them against the files and the two reports' tables. Every figure matches (6.1, 6.2); six Congress "1 in" figures differ by one in the last digit because the file's mean and sd are rounded to six decimals.

- The Senate's `P_decides_given_tie_if_VP_D` / `_if_VP_R` fields, and the presidential-year Senate seats after D2's Vice-President change lands (then rerun `c3`).
- Monte Carlo for the House in 2006–2016 and 2020 (Fourier covers every year; Monte Carlo 2018, 2022 and 2024).
- My rebuild of D2's fitting rows: governor 232 of 232, Senate 319 of 319, House 3,787 of 3,805 (the House specials on the old Congress's lines, which D2 reports). The coefficients reproduce to the third decimal and the governor forecast to the rounding; the Senate and House refits in 3.4 are between my own two fits on the same rows, so the ratios stand.
- Silver's 24 October 2008 forecast and the Economist's `p_decisive_vote` code: not reachable; what would settle each is named above.
- No subagent was used; every figure here is from the scripts.

## 4. Files

- `checks/m2/m2lib.py` — the independent implementation (densities, t4 at sd/√2, the own presidential convolution, the lattice sum, window weights, the Fourier count of electoral votes and seats, importance-sampling Monte Carlo, the readers).
- `checks/m2/c1_recompute.py` (item 1), `c2_college.py` (item 2, the College, the Economist's draws), `c3_chambers.py` (item 2, the chambers, 538 × own), `c4_congress.py` (item 3, Congress and governors), `c4b_president_538.py` (item 3, the presidency's horizon and scale, 538's rule), `c5_published.py` (item 4), `c6_record.py` (item 5), `c7_first_screen.py` (item 6).

Fetched with `curl -sSL`, one request every few seconds, no refusals.

*Written 2026-11-07 09:50 UTC by M2 on `claude/project-thread-pbr8zl`. What was checked: `data/congress/` and `data/governor/` as D2 left them after #38 and #51 (`main` at 00961e4), and D3's levels `data/state-upper/`, `data/state-lower/`, `data/local/` and `data/measure/` as of #46 and #49 (merged at 887d000). The standard is `model/MODEL.md`, `model/MATH.md` and D3's `data/local/REPORT.md` where it writes the method. Nothing outside `checks/` is changed; no data and no pipeline. Chosen: nothing.*

**Reproduce.** `sh checks/m2/run_all.sh` runs c8–c15 (about 20 minutes) into `checks/m2/OUTPUT.txt`; each script's output begins at a `##### <script>` line, and the citations below name the script's numbered section (c10 §10.6, c15 §15.5), which that line's block holds. The second pass's scripts import nothing from `pipeline/` or `model/`: `m2fit.py` is my own refit of D2's method (rows, stages, k, λ, the near-tie factor, the variants, the robustness test), `m2d3.py` my own reader and formulas for D3's levels (the like-race density, the exact sum at sd_eq, the measures' threshold form, the structure of a ballot). Sources, fetched with `curl -sSL` outside the repository and hashed on each run: Cha's candidates file, MEDSL's Senate file, The Downballot's lines sheets and Klarner's contest file (`klarner_202slers_uoa_contest20230810.csv`, sha256 21cbaeac…). One request every few seconds, no refusals.

## 5. Summary of the second pass

1. **Congress and the governors after #38 and #51 reproduce**: every p block (Senate 992, House 8,905, governors 1,028) from its own inputs to 2 × 10⁻¹⁵; every year's fit to 3 × 10⁻⁶; the near-tie factors, k and λ of record exactly; P(decides | tie) in every Senate year to 7 × 10⁻¹⁴, the Vice-President's branches included, and in the House to 3 × 10⁻⁷; π for 2024 is 0.5295, the model's own College chance with Maine's and Nebraska's districts, which closes the first pass's reading. D2's #58 (09:38) added the near-even note to the House's and the Senate's p blocks and renumbered REPORT's sections; no p, result or fit moved and `backtest.json` is as #55 left it, so items 1 and 2 stand as checked.
2. **D2's adoption tests reproduce cell for cell** (1,702 numbers, the largest difference 1.6 × 10⁻⁴ in a sum over 3,000 races) and the adoptions of record (none / none / none; the near-tie scheme level / none / none) follow the rule as written, once the robustness test is counted as the fourth condition for the variants (#51) and for the near-tie schemes (#53, with round 5's cells added to their test). Three decisions rest on one cell or one year: House shrinkage passes the three tests by 0.001 of an expected count and fails the robustness test; the governors' t4 passes the three and fails without 2010, 2018 or 2020; the Senate's near-tie level scheme, adopted under #51's record, fails round 5's 48–52% cell in the 10–20 point band by 0.28 of an expected count (none 5.52, level 5.80, 5 seen) and fails without 2012, 2014, 2020 or 2024, its band scheme without 2024, so the Senate carries no near-tie factor as of #53, which D2 did while this pass ran and which my implementation of the rule reproduces verdict for verdict. The House's level scheme is robust to every year.
3. **k, a reading, nothing chosen**: out of sample the forecasts are too wide (z rms House 0.87, Senate 0.82, governors 0.91 under the record). A k fitted on the last one to three years only tightens them (z rms 0.93–1.00) and raises the House's and Senate's log score, but moves every near-tie count further from what was seen, because a narrower forecast puts less density at ½ for the seats that are 5 to 20 points from even; the near-tie factor is the record's answer to that, and the two pull against each other.
4. **D3's every p reproduces from its recorded inputs** by my own implementation (state-upper 20,467 blocks, state-lower 71,655, local 7,752, measures 65), and the one finding of the run of 09:40 is fixed: the pipeline's exact sum cut the forecast to (0, 1) and renormalised (`model/math/common.py`), which MATH did not say and which, for a like-race class whose density stands in for a wide normal, raised p by 1/mass, by × 1.99 at the worst (1994-state-lower-NH-Cheshire_12) and in 503 like-race exact blocks beyond the rounding. Reported at 08:58; the orchestrator's reading (09:03) was that the defect is D3's route's, and D3's #59 (on `main` at 09:53) multiplies the sum by the normal's mass on its grid and records the mass as `p.exact_mass`. Every like-race exact block of the four levels (4,310) now equals my plain sum within 2 × 10⁻⁵ and its mass my Φ(hi) − Φ(lo) within 10⁻⁵ (10.3). The partisan route's exact sums keep the cut at mean ± 0.5 (at most 0.4% on p, 1.2% on the t4), as the orchestrator's reading keeps it for a forecast of the share.
5. **D3's pools and fits rebuilt from the sources** agree: the state levels' like-race classes and counts in all 16,747 contests (16,156 of Klarner's years, 591 of 2024); MEDSL's local route in all 4,186 and, since #59's reading, North Carolina's in all 1,750 (the 656 that differed at 09:40 came from 35 November contests whose name was on a ballot earlier that year, which the State Board's route reads as contests after an earlier round and leaves out of its pool, and my pool had held: 10.5); the measures in all 65; route (a)'s fit on all years exactly as REPORT §2 and its back-test table as §3. **Neither local calibration factor is of record since #59, and D3's robustness test of record reproduces cell for cell** (10.9): the like-race factors (0.786 for 2022, 0.759 for 2024) reproduce exactly; the test over the scored years 2022 and 2024 (2,374 contests, 217 seen, expected 299.6 → 222.5, deviance 43.7 → 16.9) improves the whole but moves three tested office-type cells further from what was seen (local judge, other local office, sheriff or constable), so it fails; without 2022 the same; without 2024 it passes; the partisan factor (0.903) improves 2024's whole (59.8 → 54.0 against 54 seen) but the council cell, and has no second year to leave out. Each p a factor would have moved carries `calibration_not_of_record` with the factor and the p it would give: 3,336 blocks (110 of 2022, 3,226 of 2024), every one as `calibration-local.json` and my arithmetic (10.2).
6. **Three more readings for the orchestrator**: (a) withdrawn: my reading of 09:34 that `pipeline/local.py partisan_rows` picks route (a)'s rows by who finished first and second was wrong; its helper returns every named candidate but the write-ins, and the rows are the single-seat generals with exactly one Democrat and one Republican among them, as REPORT §2 says (D3's #59 renamed the helper `on_ballot` and counted the 400 contests with a third candidate ahead as rows). What differs between the pipeline's rows and mine is 30 contests where Klarner's contest-level flags (`dcand`, `rcand`) disagree with its own candidate rows [stated, D3's REPORT]; the chamber lean differs where they sit (10.8), which is why 13 of my means are beyond 5 × 10⁻³ of the record's, and no p moves with it; (b) the boundary route (c), checked for the first time: calibrated as a whole (1,410 expected in the class windows against 1,413 seen; 17.4 margins of 0 or 1 against 20) but by size the small races are under-forecast and the large over-forecast (deciding pair under 3,000 votes: 205 expected, 234 seen; over 30,000: 356, 298), since the class density knows no size; (c) D3's point 1 holds as he put it: the size bin of the count itself is the last count's in 76% of rows and s_e differs by 4% in the median where it is not.

## 7. Reasoning of the second pass

### 7.1 Items 1 and 2: Congress after #38 and #51, and D2's tests

`c8_recompute2.py` reads every contest of `data/congress/` and `data/governor/` and recomputes each p block's chain from the file's own numbers with `m2lib`: f(½) from the mean and sd (normal, or t4 at sd/√2), the near-tie multiplier F/(1 + (F − 1)q), p = f(½)/(2n) × multiplier, the event, the control p; and each year's chamber block from its fit (c·z, k s_a √(1 + z(Z′Z)⁻¹z′), the shrunk mean). Everything reproduces to machine precision (7.1), which is what it should be: these are closed forms on recorded inputs. `c9_fit2.py` and `m2fit.py` rebuild D2's rows from Cha, MEDSL, The Downballot and the files, with the House specials on the lines of t − 2 as the files have them since #51, and refit every year: the coefficients agree to 10⁻⁷, the k and λ to 10⁻⁷, the near-tie factors to 10⁻⁵, and every contest's mean to 10⁻⁶ (7.2). `c12_control2.py` repeats the first pass's Fourier count with the Vice-President's branches: every year to 10⁻¹³ (7.3). The 2024 π is now the College chance with the districts (0.5295), the first pass's reading 3.5(5) settled by D2's #51. `c13_governors2.py` repeats 3.1 after the last-race term: the share calibration holds and the 0 within 5,000 votes stands (P 0.009 on 2006–2024; over 1986–2024 5 against 10.4, P 0.052). The reading of the first pass stands: the count at 5,000 is the unlucky line, not the forecast's middle.

`c10_backtest2.py` reproduces D2's four tests from my own refit (8.1) and then asks whether the record's adoptions follow the rule as REPORT §0.1, 2.6 and 2.7 write it (8.2). They do, once the fourth condition D2 added in #51 is counted: the governors' t4 passes the three tests on the full years and fails the leave-one-year-out test without 2010, 2018 or 2020, and D2 adopted none. 8.3 is the reading that matters for the orchestrator: the House's shrinkage decision sits on 0.001 of an expected count in its tightest safe cell and fails the robustness test, so its rejection is right by the rule and arbitrary by the margin; and the Senate's near-tie level scheme, adopted as of #51's record, fails the same robustness test without 2020 (its band without 2024). The record then applied the robustness test to the variants, not to the near-tie schemes; D2's #53 (09:10) applied it to both, with round 5's cells added to the schemes' test, and the Senate now carries no near-tie factor, the House its level scheme. `m2fit.near_tie_test` carries that rule since this pass merged #53 and reproduces every verdict of `backtest.json`'s `round_5_cells`, `leave_one_year_out` and `robust` (324 numbers, 0 beyond tolerance): the Senate's level scheme fails the 48–52% cell of the 10–20 point band on the full years (none 5.52, level 5.80, 5 seen) and without 2012, 2014 and 2024, and the safe seats' 49–51% count without 2020. I note that the near-tie test's cells for the Senate hold 5 to 18 seen, so one year's absence changes the count by a quarter.

### 7.2 Item 3: k

`c11_k_reading.py` scores, on earlier years only and in the way D2's variants were scored, four alternatives to the record's k (which is fitted on all earlier years): k from the last one, two or three years' residuals, and a trend. The record's forecasts are too wide out of sample (9.1); the last-year fits tighten them to z rms 0.93–1.00 and raise the House's and Senate's log score (9.2). But every one of them moves the 49–51% count and the safe-band counts further from what was seen, and the mechanism is plain: in a seat 10 points from even a narrower sd lowers the density at ½, and the record's near-tie counts already run below what was seen there (House 0.10–0.20 band: 14.2 expected against 16 seen; Senate 3.0 against 4). The near-tie factor is the record's correction for exactly that, and a tighter k would have to be fitted together with it. The orchestrator's word (09:03) is that D2 runs only its untested half-life variant, then the incumbency variants, under the robustness test; the scores here are on the same tests so they can be read beside those.

### 7.3 Item 4: D3's levels

**Every p from its inputs.** `c14_d3_recompute.py` reads each contest of the four levels through `m2d3.slim` and checks every recorded number against my own: the pair's n and share, the window and its widening (k ≥ 10), N ≥ 100, q to five decimals, f(½) = q/(2h), p = f(½)/(2n), the event, the method rule (exact where n < 1,000 or n sd_eq² < 6.25), for the partisan route the normal and t4 densities within an envelope of the file's 4-decimal mean and sd, and since #59 the mass on every like-race exact block and the factor, key, fitting years and value of every `calibration_not_of_record` block (10.2). All pass. The run of 09:40 had one finding (10.3): the pipeline's lattice sum integrated a normal cut to (0, 1) and renormalised, and MATH §1.2 did not say so. For Congress this is invisible (the first pass noted it as a possible reading, × 1.31 for the farthest seat). For a like-race class it was not: the class's f is a measured density, sd_eq is the normal that has that density at ½, and a class with f = 1.2 has sd_eq 0.33, so a third of the normal's mass lies outside (0, 1) and the renormalised sum was 1.5 times the plain one; 503 like-race exact blocks were affected beyond the rounding, every one by exactly 1/mass. The orchestrator's reading (09:03) assigned the fix to D3's route and kept `common.py`'s cut for forecasts of a share (MATH §1's note, #52); D3's #59 multiplies the renormalised sum by the normal's mass on the sum's grid and records the mass, and every exact block of the like-race routes now equals the plain sum of MATH §1.2 at sd_eq, the mass with it.

**The pools.** `c15_d3_refit.py` rebuilds every like-race pool from the files alone (the state levels from Klarner's contests, the local pools from MEDSL's and the State Board's) and from Klarner's contest file (route (a)). The classes and counts reproduce exactly in every pool (10.4, 10.5, 10.6). Three conventions had to be read from the pipeline to get there, and all three are worth a line in D3's REPORT (the third is there since #59): a file's "built by" names the build that wrote the year's file, so a State Board contest of May 2018 sits in a file `local_medsl.py` wrote while its forecast is North Carolina's route's (the route is the source and the date: MEDSL's, or the State Board's November contests in MEDSL's place); a year whose state fails its check (California's and Wyoming's 2022, Indiana's, Louisiana's and New Jersey's 2024) is left out of every pool that year, which the files say only contest by contest in the reason; and the State Board's route reads a November contest whose name was on a ballot earlier that year (a nonpartisan primary) as a contest after an earlier round, leaves it out of its pool and computes no p for it on its own dates, while MEDSL's route writes the same contest as a general with p. The run of 09:40 had read those 35 contests as generals in the State Board's pool too, which is why that pool ran 1 to 17 rows long in 656 contests; read as the route reads them, all 1,750 agree. Whether the State Board's route should read them as generals, as MEDSL's does, is a choice D3's REPORT §9 leaves to the orchestrator; it moves which contests a route takes, not any p of record.

**Route (a).** The fit on all years and the back-test by earlier years reproduce REPORT §2 and §3 to the printed digits (10.7). Per contest, my fit on earlier years only gives the record's mean within 5 × 10⁻⁴ in 97.6% of 72,466 contests, which is the size of difference a fit on rows that differ by a few of ninety thousand makes; the 13 beyond 5 × 10⁻³ are all in chamber-years whose lean differs. At 09:34 I read the lean difference as the pipeline selecting the rows by who finished first and second; that reading was wrong (10.8), and D3's #59 shows it: the helper lists every named candidate but the write-ins, and `partisan_rows` keeps the single-seat generals with exactly one Democrat and one Republican on that list, which is the ballot's rule. The rows differ because mine come from Klarner's contest-level flags and the pipeline's from its candidate rows, and the two disagree in 30 contests [stated, D3]; by chamber-year, my rows and the files' candidate rows under the ballot rule differ in 24 chamber-years by 96 rows of 91,018. Nothing for the orchestrator here.

**The #31 factor** (10.9). Since #59 neither local factor is of record: D3 applies the orchestrator's robustness test (08:35) as a rule in `local_medsl.py` (every scored year with its own factor, fitted on the earlier years; adopted only if the whole improves and no tested cell, with 5 or more seen or expected, is made worse, and still so with any one scored year left out), and the like-race factor fails it on the cells (local judge, other local office, sheriff or constable, in the full test and without 2022), while the partisan factor fails the council cell and has no second year. My implementation of that rule gives the record's every number: the factors, the fitting counts by office type, the contests, seen, expected and deviance before and after, the cells, and the verdicts. The p each factor would have given is kept in `calibration_not_of_record` (3,336 blocks), which 10.2 checks. The arithmetic behind the verdict is as at 09:40: the like-race factor rests on 2020 (1,812 contests, 249 seen against 317 expected), the only year with the weight to fit one, and 2022 (110 contests, North Carolina's November) cannot stand in for it.

**The boundary route** (10.10). Route (c) had not been checked against outcomes. It is calibrated as a whole and by class, and the record's `backtest.json` cells reproduce from my pools. Two things the whole hides: by the deciding pair's size the forecast runs 12% low under 3,000 votes and 20% high over 30,000, the same shape route (a) answers with s_e by size bin, and the class density could too (the orchestrator chooses); and the exact margins (0 or 1 vote) run above the smooth forecast in the multi-seat classes (4+ seats: 7 seen against 2.9), which I read as slates: candidates of one party in a multi-seat race often carry identical counts, so the k-th and (k + 1)-th tie more often than a density predicts [inferred].

## 8. What the second pass did not do

- D3's Congress-style control stage: there is none at these levels.
- D3's November deciding-round PR and the size-bin test of route (c) (10.10): the orchestrator's word (09:53) is that they come later and are left to a third pass on his word.
- D2's #55 variants (the half-life k, Cha's incumbency codes 2 and 3 as a test): not reimplemented; #55 adopted neither and changed no p, and the adoptions of record reproduce without them.
- Any choice. Items 3 and 4 are readings; the orchestrator and D2 and D3 decide.

## 9. Files of the second pass

- `checks/m2/m2fit.py` (D2's method, my own: rows from Cha, MEDSL, The Downballot; the stages; k, λ, F; the variants; the robustness test), `checks/m2/m2d3.py` (D3's levels: the reader, the density, sd_eq, the exact sum's rule, the ballot's structure).
- `c8_recompute2.py` (7.1), `c9_fit2.py` (7.2), `c12_control2.py` (7.3), `c13_governors2.py` (7.4), `c10_backtest2.py` (8.1–8.3), `c11_k_reading.py` (9.1–9.2), `c14_d3_recompute.py` (10.1–10.3), `c15_d3_refit.py` (10.4–10.11).
- Sources in `~/.cache/vote-m2/`: `cha_candidates_2006-2024.dta`, `medsl_1976-2024-senate-state.csv`, `downballot_sheet_2006.csv`, `_2018.csv`, `_2022.csv`, `klarner_202slers_uoa_contest20230810.csv` (sha256 21cbaeac…); each script prints the hash it checked.

# Third pass (the brief of 10:55, amended in #64)

*Written 2026-11-07 by M2 on `claude/project-thread-pbr8zl` (PR #69). What was checked: the orchestrator's two-test rule for the near-even notes (item 0) and D2's #67 that applied it; the first pass's scripts on today's files (item 1); D3's #60 (North Carolina's November deciding rounds), #61 (route (c)'s size bins), #62 and #68 (Louisiana 2024 and its party committees), #65 and #66 (New Jersey 2024 by county and the ballots-cast bound), #70 (Louisiana 2023), #72 (North Carolina's rounds keyed by place), #73 (office types read by the office), D2's #74 (the odd-year governors), D3's #75 (2023 feeding the later years) D3's #77 (local questions' thresholds read from state law), D2's #76 (New Jersey and Virginia 2025, Rhode Island 2010, New Jersey 2021's last race) and #78 (the governor incumbency codes), D3's #79 (the lean notes under the rule of 11:01 as amended at 13:52), #80 (his fixes from 10.9) and #81 (step (a): each held statewide question's own ballot text), as `main` stood at 8b794e7 (merged into this branch at dc727b1; what lands after it goes to a fourth pass, the orchestrator, 15:12). D2's #71 (the Census population of each district) changed no p file [measured from git]. The standard is `model/MODEL.md`, `model/MATH.md`, D3's `data/local/REPORT.md` and the orchestrator's rulings as the PRs quote them. Nothing outside `checks/` is changed; no data and no pipeline. Chosen: nothing.*

**Reproduce.** `sh checks/m2/run_all.sh` now runs c1–c30 (about half an hour) into `checks/m2/OUTPUT.txt`: c1–c7 on today's files, c17 (where their output differs from the first pass's run of record at d11cae2), c8–c15, then the third pass's c16 and c18–c30 (the quote checks need the pages fetched first: `sh checks/m2/fetch_law_pages.sh` for c27, `fetch_governor_rosters.sh` for c28, `fetch_ballot_texts.sh` for c30). Citations below name the script's numbered section (c19 §19.3), which its `##### <script>` block holds. The third pass's scripts import nothing from `pipeline/` or `model/`; where a rule of D3's had to be applied (the State Board's round words, Louisiana's runoff class, MEDSL's resolve rule, New Jersey's gate), it was read from the pipeline and written again in the script, and the script says so. Sources, fetched with `curl -sSL` outside the repository (`~/.cache/vote-m2/`), 4 s apart, one request at a time: Louisiana's four 2024 and seven 2023 workbooks and the posted multi-parish results of each 2023 date (`la_sos/`), two statute pages (`la_sos/law/`), New Jersey's three Official Lists, MEDSL's 2024 New Jersey precinct file and four county clerks' files (`nj/`); Morris County's clerk refused (HTTP 403, not retried); the 87 law pages the local-threshold rules cite (`law_local/`, ncleg.gov 43 and legis.la.gov 44, one request per 5 s per host, all HTTP 200); the ten governor rosters D2's incumbency table cites (`governor_rosters/`, one request per 5 s, all HTTP 200); the four rules read in step (a) and 15 ballot-text sources (`ballot_texts/`, one request per 5 s, all HTTP 200).

## 10. The third pass

**Summary.** Every p the sixteen PRs computed or moved reproduces from my own pools and formulas: #60's 1,734 moved p row by row, #61's test cell for cell, Louisiana's 9 p of 2024 and 29 of 2023, New Jersey's 54, #72's 461 moved and 53 new p, #73's 13,390-row moves file row by row with its 40 largest moves and 129 new p recomputed, #74's 29 odd-year governor p with their 20 model blocks rebuilt from even-year fits and their back-test, #75's 141 + 62 + 43 moved p with the ×1.42 explained (the window rule switching on its tenth row), #77's 800 local-question p from my own reading of its 55 rules and my own pools, with all 144 of the rules' quotes found word for word on the pages I fetched, #78's 387 moved governor p (every even-year one recomputed from my own refit with the fixed codes, the 17 fixes checked against the files and the ten rosters' 28 quotes, the House's switch test and its 8 leave-one-year-out sets reproduced), #76's two 2025 governors, #79's notes under the amended rule (every verdict the record's), #80's 867 moved local p with the superintendents, Virginia's special and Montana's ×0.5, and #81's 67 statewide measure p from my own pool, with every one of its 116 questions' rules re-read from the ballot texts and the state's rules and 31 quotes found on the pages I fetched. Findings went to the orchestrator as found, and the record has answered six: the State Board's rounds were keyed on the name with no place (10.3; #72, 10.8), D3's REPORT said 52 New Jersey p where the file holds 54 (10.6), 2023 was kept out of 2024's pools (10.7; #75, 10.11), New Jersey 2021's last race (10.10; #76, 10.13), Virginia's 2018 special and the superintendents (10.9; #80, 10.15) and the local classes' note (10.12; #79, 10.14). Open for the orchestrator: one New Jersey question recorded as an unopposed office with p 0 (10.6); D3's REPORT line on the 103 p no longer computed (10.9); the governors' note reading the other way on the files' forecasts (10.1); #80's "1,720 p moved", which is 867 moved and 854 whose not-of-record factor moved, and 26 state superintendents of public instruction typed as local officers (10.15); and two North Carolina questions at a margin of exactly 10 votes that D3's floating-point share form leaves out of the (v) count (10.14). No p differs from my own by more than a factor of two; the largest relative difference on a like-race p is 4.5 × 10⁻⁶, on a partisan p 1.7 × 10⁻³ (the exact sum's cut, allowed since c14).

### 10.1 Item 0: the near-even notes under the two-test rule, and D2's #67

`c16_notes.py` applies the rule as the orchestrator gave it (a test counts where 5 or more are seen or expected; a note stands where the two tests that count lean the same way; the chance is one-sided, exact from the contests' own p with Poisson beside). On the back-test's own rows (set A, the record the notes cite): the House stands ((s) 105 against 87.5 within 1 point, chance 0.030; (v) 24 against 16.9 within 1,000 votes, 0.057); the Senate goes ((s) 17 against 11.1, more; (v) 4 against 5.1 at 10,000 votes, fewer); the governors go on his numbers ((s) 17 against 15.8; (v) 6 against 9.7 at 10,000); the statewide measures stand ((s) 52 against 36.4 on #67's files — 54 against 38.1 on today's, after #75 (10.11) —, chance 0.005; (v) 10 against 6.0 at 20,000) (c16 §A–C; since #79 c16 applies the rule as amended at 13:52, with the same verdicts: 10.14). On the files' forecasts of record (set B) the House and the Senate read the same and the governors read the other way ((s) 14 against 14.8, (v) 4 against 8.5: both fewer, so the note would stand); the orchestrator ruled on set A. After #67: `history.json`'s `lean_note` block carries set A's numbers for all three and `governor_note` is absent; the House's p blocks carry the note (2,514 of 4,411, none of the 1,897 where none is expected), the Senate's 351 and the governors' 562 carry none — as the rule (c16 §D). Sent 11:16 UTC.

The differences are the later PRs' own: D1's #33 (fallback units and W in the fourth decimal; the average voter's p in 2016 from 6.2764 × 10⁻⁸ to 6.3033 × 10⁻⁸ and in 2020 from 1.5455 × 10⁻⁸ to 1.5512 × 10⁻⁸), D2's #29 (c3's 538-first years, by design), #38 and #51 (c4's fit on all years from 1978 and 1982 rather than the first pass's windows: the governors' lean coefficient 0.2921 on 541 rows against the first pass's 0.4113 on 232, and the leave-one-cycle-out score against the earlier-cycles fit, 7.55 × 10⁻²), #53's near-tie multiplier (c7's first screen) and the renamed input key c3 had stopped on. c5 is identical. Every check still passes (c1–c7, c17).

### 10.11 #75: 2023 feeds the later years (`c26_feed_2023.py`)

- **The moves file** (`moves-2023-feed.json`, 248 rows) against my diff of every contest between #74 and #75 (61 files): the 141 P, 62 N and 43 measure P rows are exactly the p that changed, with p before and after as the commits hold them; the two tie rows are the only other `result` changes; not listed, as the file says: 2,207 `calibration_not_of_record`, 383 reasons' counts of like races, 52 + 7 code stamps and 22 measure notes (c26 §26.1).
- **The ×1.42 class explained.** "local council, commission or board, one major party against others, 2 candidates", 2024: without 2023 the class has N 219 like races, 7 within 1 point of ½ and 13 within 2 (the first window holding the rule's 10), so h = 0.02, q = 13/219, f = 1.484. With 2023's 18 rows (3 within 1 point, 4 within 2, 11 within 5) it has 237 and 10 within 1 point — exactly the minimum — so h narrows to 0.01, q = 10/237, f = 2.110: ×1.4216, the record's move (×1.33 to ×1.42 over the 141; every (N, k, h, q) equal from my pool with 2023, none without). At the same window 0.02 the move would be ×1.21 (17/237 against 13/219). The rise is the window rule switching on the tenth row: one fewer near-even row in 2023 and the window would have stayed at 0.02 (c26 §26.2).
- **The 62 new p.** Their class, "two or more of one major party, 2 candidates", has 67 like races without 2023 and 116 with (49 of 2023's, Louisiana's runoffs); all 62 (N, k, h, q) equal; by state WA 23, LA 21, MS 8, NY 5, others 5 (c26 §26.3).
- **The 43 statewide measures.** The class's like measures 215 → 223 with 2023's 8 amendments; all 43 (N, k, h, q) equal and the density at the threshold within 1.1 × 10⁻⁶; the ratio 0.9641 in every one. **The note at its new counts** (c16 on today's files, the ruling of 11:01): 259 measures scored (2018 87, 2022 45, 2023 8, 2024 119); (s) 54 against 38.09 within 5 points (chance 0.005); (v) at 20,000 votes, the smallest line with 5 or more expected, 10 against 6.21 (chance 0.093); both count and lean the same way: the note stands, and the 43 carried "54 against 38.1" (c26 §26.4); since #79 they carry the amended rule's "11 against 6.8" (10.14).
- **The two ties.** Cameron's police juror 5 (178–178) and Opelousas's alderman A (224–224) carry `result.tie` and `tie_settled` (a new election on 2023-12-16; La. R.S. 18:512(C) quoted, with the fourth Saturday), and the 12-16 contests hold the same candidates (c26 §26.5). **The robustness test:** no adoption changes; leaving 2024 out now scores 2022 and 2023 (128 contests), council's cell is tested (5 seen) and its factor moves the expected count 4.6 → 3.7, away from the count, so that set fails — as D3 reported before the PR (c26 §26.6).

- **The rules file.** 55 rules (North Carolina 39, Louisiana 16), every one scoped local with a pattern that compiles; t = ½ on 54 and none on the acreage tax (compound); 17 hold, 1 advisory, 2 compound; marks as D3's table (NC 22 stated, 17 inferred; LA 8 and 8). Indiana's 33 questions are "not read" in the states block (c27 §27.1).

- **The quotes against their pages.** The rules cite 144 quotes (the rules' own 55, `also_cited` 38, `supporting` 51) on 87 distinct URLs. I fetched all 87 (`fetch_law_pages.sh`; ncleg.gov 43, legis.la.gov 44, all HTTP 200) and found every quote word for word with whitespace, quote marks, dashes and entities normalised (§27.2). One page holds only a repeal notice (G.S. 105-600, cited as such).
- **Every p recomputed**, not a sample: each North Carolina and Louisiana local question counted whole (870 and 619) takes, by my own reading of the rules file (the first rule in file order whose `match`, `match_also` and `dates` its title fits, holds and advisory and compound rules giving a reason), the rule the record cites; the pool (NC: the State Board's local questions of earlier years, 32 of 2008 to 614 by 2020; LA: 2023's 299 for 2024) gives the record's N, window and count in every case; all 800 p equal (f_t, n and p within 10⁻⁵, the moves row too). The 374 NC and 315 LA reasons for no p reproduce one for one: 216 other bonds held, 77 with under 100 like questions (0, 32, 71 in 2008–2011), 63 held for a local act, 7 and 11 not covered, 4 transit compound, 287 of Louisiana 2023 with no earlier class, the rest as D3 lists them (§27.3). D3's `n_questions` on the two bond rules (278 each) and the two school-tax rules (4 each) counts the pattern's matches, not the rule taken (62 + 216, 3 + 1). The p's range and median as D3 states them.
- **The out-of-sample counts.** Rebuilt from my recomputation, `at_threshold_local` (LA 2024: 14.2/14, 7.1/8, 14.2/14, 39.7/41; NC general dates: 6.5/5, 6.5/5, 15.6/11, 38.9/30) and `questions_at_threshold` (NC other dates: 19.6/23, 10.9/15, 28.8/31, 65.6/82) equal the files and REPORT §17's table, year by year (§27.4). Louisiana's and 120 of NC's other-dates questions took the 2-point window, the rest the 1-point one.
- **Reading, nothing chosen.** None of the 800 carries a near-½ note. Under the 11:01 rule's share test, NC's other-dates class finished within 5 points 82 times against 65.6 expected (chance 0.015 of a count that far off), its general-dates class 30 against 38.9 (0.06), Louisiana 41 against 39.7; the votes test says little for questions this small (§27.5). Whether the rule reaches local classes is the orchestrator's; #79 answered it, and c27 now prints the amended rule's tests (10.14).

### 10.14 #79: the lean notes under the rule of 11:01 as amended at 13:52

The amendment: (s) within 1 point of the threshold; (v) within k votes, k the smallest of 10, 100, 1,000, 5,000, 10,000, 20,000 and 50,000 at which 5 or more are expected, and (v) does not count where its line holds more than half the pool; a test counts with 5 or more seen or expected; by pool, each build's own back-test. c16, c25 and c27 apply it now (no pool reaches the half-the-pool clause).

- **The federal and state levels** (c16 §D): the House stands ((s) 105 against 87.5 within 1 point; (v) 24 against 16.9 at 1,000 votes), the Senate goes ((s) 17 against 11.1, more; (v) 4 against 5.1 at 10,000, fewer), the governors go on the back-test's own rows ((s) 17 against 15.6; (v) 6 against 9.6 at 10,000; the record's own rows 15.8 and 9.7): the record's three verdicts. **The statewide measures** stand: (s) 11 against 6.84 within 1 point (chance 0.085), (v) 10 against 6.21 at 20,000 votes (0.093); the record's `lean_notes_statewide` reads 11 against 6.8 and 10 against 6.2, and the notes on the p carry those figures (c16 §C, c26 §26.4; unchanged by #81, which reads the note near ½ on every statewide question whose class has earlier years, 10.16).
- **The odd-year governors** (c25 §25.4): (s) 6 against 3.17 within 1 point; (v) at 50,000 votes, the smallest line with 5 or more expected (10,000: 1.24; 20,000: 2.48), 9 against 6.16; both count and lean the same way: the note stands and reads "may be low", as the record's; the 31 p carry it with "6 against 3.2", the 420 even-year p none.
- **The local questions** (c27 §27.5): Louisiana 2024's class (304 p): (s) 8 against 7.12, more; (v) at 10 votes 9 against 17.57, fewer: the note goes (the record: none). North Carolina's general-dates class (190): (s) 5 against 6.51, fewer; (v) at 100 votes 19 against 17.36, more: goes (the record: none). North Carolina's other-dates class (306): (s) 15 against 10.90, more; (v) at 10 votes 25 against 21.90, more: stands, and its 306 p carry the note (the record: stands on both tests). **One reading:** the record's (v) count for that class is 23 where mine is 25. Two questions finished with a margin of exactly 10 votes; D3's share form, |s − t| ≤ k / (2(yes + no)) in floating point, drops them at the boundary where the integer margin keeps them. The lean is "more" either way; no p and no note differs (§27.5).

# Fourth pass (the orchestrator's word of 15:50)

*Written 2026-11-07 by M2 on `claude/project-thread-pbr8zl`. What was checked: D3's #82 (the statewide questions MEDSL files with local offices, joined from the state's own list; the rule cited by who proposed the question; the near-even note taken off a p at a threshold other than ½), #84 (the certified count wins) and #85 (one-county titles counted whole where the place lies wholly in the county), with `main` at bdac161 (#69 merged) as the pass's fixed end. The orchestrator's words: "For each, the moves file should equal your own diff, and every moved p should be recomputed from your own pool." Anything merged after bdac161 goes to a fifth pass on his word.*

**Reproduce.** `sh checks/m2/run_all.sh` now runs c1–c33 (about 35 minutes) into `checks/m2/OUTPUT.txt`; the fourth pass's c31–c33 read the pages `sh checks/m2/fetch_fourth_pass.sh` fetches first (`curl -sSL` into `~/.cache/vote-m2/fourth/`, outside the repository, one request every 5 s, a host that refuses not asked again; 26 pages, all HTTP 200: the Census Bureau's two 2020 files, the eleven state lists the record cites with Washington's two results sub-pages and its canvass PDF, and ten pages of the threshold rules that carry a proposer; then MEDSL's own 2018 Louisiana, Arizona and Washington files and its 2020 local file, read by a blob-less `git clone` and `git show` at the commits `pipeline/intake/medsl_precinct.py` names, as the pipeline reads them). The scripts import nothing from `pipeline/` or `model/`; where a rule of D3's had to be applied (`rule_for`, `proposer_of` and `_one` of `pipeline/measures.py`; `match` of `pipeline/places.py`; MEDSL's TOTAL-row rule of `medsl_precinct.py`), it was read from the pipeline and written again in the script, which says so, and a second reading by my own tests follows it. Citations below name the script's numbered section (c33 §33.3), which its `##### <script>` block in OUTPUT.txt holds.

## 11. The fourth pass

### 11.1 #82: the statewide questions filed local, the citations by proposer, the notes (`c31_statewide_filed_local.py`)

- **The moves files against my diff** of the 67 record files changed between 8b794e7 and cbc4469 (§31.1) [measured]: 35 new records, 1,420 gone, 236 changed. The 35 new are exactly the joins' ids and the 1,420 gone exactly the lines they replace (`moves-statewide-filed-local.json`); its 191 `other_changes` give p before and after as the records do (191 of 191, on the keys the file gives); `moves-citations-notes.json`'s 75 records equal the records in every field (citation before and after, the `matched` text, the note before and after with its t, the reason, the proposer). Read the other way, the 56 records whose threshold or ballot-text block changed are its 56 citation records, the 18 whose near-½ note went are its 18 note records, and the one whose reason now cites two rules (Nebraska's 2022 Amendment 1) is its one `not_computed` record. The 26 records left are North Carolina's (2018: 19, 2024: 7), changed only in their `p.commit` stamp, which the moves file leaves out by its own word.
- **The 35 joins** (§31.2) [measured]: for each, the joined record's votes are the sum, answer by answer, of the lines it replaced as the records held them at 8b794e7; the lines joined, the yes share and the total votes follow (35 of 35). Where the state's page prints a total (17), my MEDSL-less-state and `agrees` equal the record's and the moves file's. Then at source: for the five state-years whose MEDSL file the pipeline reads by git, every line equals MEDSL's own precinct rows added up over the line's counties (one precinct's votes for one answer its TOTAL row where it has one, else the sum of its modes, as the pipeline reads them), and every joined total equals: 2018 Louisiana 384 lines, Arizona 15, Washington 2; 2020 Alabama 402, Montana 56; no redacted count among them. The 2016 (Arkansas 4, Florida 1, Rhode Island 7) and 2024 (Illinois 306, Minnesota 87, Washington 156) lines come from Dataverse sets not read here: their sums stand on the records at 8b794e7.
- **Each on its state's list** (§31.3) [measured]: the eleven list pages the record cites fetched again, and Washington's two results sub-pages (the Measures tab names the measures and prints no figures; the record's quote files name the sub-pages). Every quote the record takes from those pages is on them: 127 of 127 (the question's words 33, the type's 29, the proposer's 22, the special rule's fact 16, the list's statewide words 11, the printed totals 16), 121 word for word, one with punctuation aside, five with the spaces aside that Washington's page gives when its tags are stripped. 30 quotes come from pages the record names by a cache file only (Montana's canvass report, Alabama's sample ballot, Washington's 2024 release, Minnesota's Revisor chapter, Rhode Island's two acts, among them), with no URL in `statewide-filed-local.json` to fetch them by: counted, not checked.
- **The citations** (§31.4) [measured]: `rule_for`, `proposer_of` and `_one` written again and run on all 497 statewide questions and retentions at cbc4469. 322 take a rule: citation, `also_governs` and the `matched` line equal the record's in every one; 138 take none, with the record's reason word for word; 37 the record holds back before any rule is read (eight for redacted precincts, seven North Carolina questions off November's general, California's and Wyoming's 2022 files, and the like), as the pipeline's order has it. The proposer narrows the citation in 22 (DC 1, MI 2, MO 2, MT 6, OH 2, OK 4, OR 5); 33 records cite every rule that could govern. The 75's citations before equal the records' at 8b794e7 (48 of 48 with one). The 28 rules that carry a `proposer` against their own texts: in 25 the rule's quoted words, `applies_to` or rule name carry a word for each proposer named; in 3 (Mo. Const. art. III §52(b); Okla. Const. art. V §3 and §33(C)) the text says "referred to the people" where the list names the legislature, a legislature's referral being a referral, which I read as consistent. Thirteen of the 28 rules' pages were fetched (ten pages: DC, ME, MI, MO, MT, ND) and every quote is on its page word for word; NE, OH, OK and OR's pages were not asked (the cap of ten, and hosts D3 lists as refusing).

The back-test at those 18 questions' own thresholds from my pool: within 1 point, 0.9 expected and 0 seen; the vote test under 5 expected at every line up to 50,000 votes (0.35); neither test counts, so no note, as `lean_notes_statewide_own_threshold` records.
- **Every moved p**, not a sample (§31.6) [measured]: 132 statewide p moved (×0.934 to ×1.128) and one is new, Montana's LR-130; all 133 from my own pool of like statewide measures (c15's), (N, k, h, q, f_t, n, value) all equal, the value within 3.9 × 10⁻⁶. The joins add 26 like measures to every later pool (34 p of 2022, 8 of 2023, 90 of 2024 see N rise by 26), which is the whole of the movement. LR-130: p 1.24 × 10⁻⁶ at ½, 189 like measures, 11 within ±2 points, n 585,516, finished 50.96%.

### 11.4 What the fourth pass did not do

- MEDSL's 2016 and 2024 sets (Dataverse) were not read: the Arkansas, Florida, Rhode Island, Illinois, Minnesota and Washington 2024 joins stand on the lines as the records held them at 8b794e7, not on MEDSL's own rows.
- 30 quotes from pages the record names by a cache file only were not checked; the hosts D3 lists as refusing (Rhode Island's election sites, azsos.gov) were not asked; 15 of the 28 proposer rules' pages were not fetched (the cap of ten, and NE, OH, OK and OR's hosts); the Washington canvass is a scan and was not read by machine (no OCR here).
- The 722 whole questions were read by tests on the title and the Census rows, not by each question's ballot; towns and townships (D3's own "not done") were not read.
- **Readings, nothing chosen:** (a) `statewide-filed-local.json` names six pages by cache file only, with no URL, so a reader cannot fetch them from the record; (b) the three proposer rules whose recorded text says "referred" where the list names the legislature; (c) "City Of Perry City Tax" counted whole as Perry town's by the typed-title path, which the county guard does not cover; (d) #77's moves file (`moves-local-thresholds.json`) now differs from Louisiana's 2024 p by the ×1.0435 that `moves-one-county.json` records, which is as it should be.

# Fifth pass (the orchestrator's word of 16:53)

*Added 2026-11-07 by M2. The orchestrator at 16:53 UTC: "Fifth pass: check #86, #87 and #88, with a2a4b0a as the fixed end. Anything later goes to a sixth pass." His rulings of 16:53 on §11.4's readings: (a) D3 is to name the URL of each of the six quote pages; (b) a rule's proposer must be the one its text names; (c) Perry stands ("City Of" names the city, and that is what the county guard exists to settle). The three are closed here. Main moved bdac161 (#69) → e524840 (#86) → afbb7bd (#87) → 8406745 (#88) → a2a4b0a (#89); each check compares the records at a PR's base with the records at its merge, so nothing later than a2a4b0a is read.*

**Reproduce.** `sh checks/m2/fetch_fifth_pass.sh` fetches the sources into `~/.cache/vote-m2/fifth/` (MEDSL's Dataverse files: 2016's local and state sets, 2022's and 2024's state files; 2018's zips by blob-less `git` from MEDSL's repositories, as the pipeline reads them; the State Board's 13 `results_pct` zips; New Mexico's two pages; curl -sSL, 5 s apart; `index.txt` holds each URL, time, sha256 and size), then `sh checks/m2/run_all.sh` runs c1–c36. `checks/m2/m2medsl.py` summarizes each raw file once into `fifth/derived/` (about 20 million rows the first time) and the checks read the summaries; `m2places.py` is a verbatim copy of `pipeline/places.py` at e524840, named as such, so that the Census matching is applied here in the pipeline's own words and then tested apart from it (§12.1).

## 14. The seventh pass

### 14.1 The input-volume check, built apart from D3's (`c38_input_volume.py`)

**What it does** [measured]. For every one of the 208,518 p (5,081 state-year-source groups, §38.1), n or the contest's total is set against a count that does not come from the same file:
- §38.2 MEDSL's 120 precinct files: each President, US Senate, US House and Governor contest against D1's or D2's record (130 state-years with a file and a certified count, 99 with p resting on the file). Each precinct's rows are its named TOTAL row where it has one, else its modes added; a statewide office filed under several district codes is added up. **Subtotal rows** (`m2medsl._subtotal_rows`, written apart from the pipeline's rule): a row named TOTAL/-1/ALL whose votes equal its county's other rows added up, or a row whose name the county's other rows extend ("Allendale" beside "Allendale 1", "Allendale Provisional") and whose votes equal them, within 0.5% or 10 votes (the slack only from 50 votes). New Jersey 2024's Bergen County files 140 municipality totals as precincts under mode TOTAL (NJ-05 +79% raw, +0.02% once dropped; 1,478,257 votes over 14 contests); Idaho 2022's COUNTY TOTAL rows (+99.3% raw, −0.33% after); New Jersey 2018's Camden precincts are every one named "... DIST n TOTAL" and are real rows (the first draft of the rule, by name, dropped them: NJ-01 −67.7%).
- §38.3 Klarner's legislative returns against MEDSL's STATE SENATE and STATE HOUSE by district (2,479 chamber-years in Klarner, 200 with a MEDSL file): the two parties' lines where both sides' D and R carry 80% or more of the district (Minnesota's DFL is filed as OTHER), else every vote; a chamber-year is listed when off by more than 0.5% in aggregate, with a district off by more than 5%, or with a district at exactly 2x or 1/2 (65 chamber-years; 249 districts in `checks/m2/c38-districts.json`).
- §38.4 North Carolina's State Board files (13 general elections) against D1, D2 and Klarner; §38.5 Indiana's 2024 certified files against D1, D2 and MEDSL's Indiana file; §38.6 Louisiana's workbooks against D1 and D2 (2024 exact; 2023's governor is D2's own source, no second count); §38.7 D1's and D2's records against MEDSL's state-level President and Senate files.
- §38.8 the p with no second count in any source read, and a coarse tier for Klarner's chamber-years without a MEDSL file: the chamber's votes × N_full / (the day's statewide count × Σ seats²) against the chamber's own years of the same district count (flag above 1.5x or below 0.5x the median).
- §38.9 the list, one line per state-year off by more than 0.5% on a direct comparison: 126 state-years, `checks/m2/c38-state-years.json`.

**What touches a p, and by about what factor** [measured; the record's own figures stated]:
1. **Idaho 2022, Senate district 1** (`2022-state-upper-ID-1`): Klarner has Herndon alone, 13,064, unopposed, p 0; MEDSL has Herndon 13,064 and Steven R. Johnson 9,025 (OTHER), 59% to 41%. Sent to the orchestrator at once (21:2x). D3's #97 takes it to not computed (the canvass marks Johnson a write-in with 69% of the winner's votes; eighth pass).
2. **Vermont 2018, Chittenden Senate** (`2018-state-upper-VT-Chittenden`): the record's total 1,096,537 and n 176,093 (Ashe 163,174) against MEDSL's Ashe 44,001, Lyons 41,617, Ingram 40,751, Sirotkin 38,569: Klarner's figures 3.7x MEDSL's; p 2.33e-5 too low by that factor. Sent at once; #97 moves it ×3.49 to MEDSL's counts (eighth pass).
3. **New Hampshire 2022**: every one of Klarner's 24 Senate figures is exactly 2x MEDSL's (chamber +100.0%); the House is +54.9% in aggregate with no district matched by key. #95 put the Senate p and the matched House p on MEDSL's counts (the 22 Senate p ×1.97–2.36, the 94 House p ×1.01–2.38, by the moves file) and held 22 House contests; checked in 14.3.
4. **New York 2024, Monroe and Ontario Counties**: MEDSL's file lacks US House rows for 200 of Monroe's 659 precincts and Assembly rows for 185 of them (29.0% of the county's presidential vote), and Assembly rows for 54 of Ontario's 92 (52.8%); the President and the Senate are whole (NY-25 −31.9% against D2). The Assembly p of Monroe's districts 135–138 (n 25,504; 46,058; 34,594; 60,363; p 1.2e-5 to 2.8e-5) rest on n short by up to about 29%, so too high by up to about ×1.4; district 131 (Ontario) is split and not computed; 132, 134 and 139 are unopposed. Eight local p of Monroe County rest on the same file. A reading for D3 (the president's gate cannot see it).
5. **Klarner's districts against MEDSL's, where p rests on Klarner** (`c38-districts.json`): Michigan 2022's House 63 (43,648 vs 22,966, ×1.9) and Senate 3 (71,490 vs 30,412, ×2.35), New York 2022's Senate 56 (+54%), New Mexico 2022's House 53 (+78%), Illinois 2022's House 2 (−70%), Florida 2018's House 40 (58,557 vs 228,549; MEDSL's rows look like another contest's), Arizona 2018's House 18 (−24%), Connecticut 2018's House 1 (441 vs 4,419). Where Klarner's n is the one that is wrong, p moves by the inverse factor (Michigan's 63rd ×0.53, Illinois's 2nd ×3.3); neither side is right by default. The 2016 state file and the 2022 "local" files (Arkansas, Washington) are partial on MEDSL's side and say nothing against Klarner. D3's district-level checks (ratio off by more than 3%; uncontested against MEDSL) are next in its queue; this list is to be set against them.
6. **Washington 2022, US House 8** −8.3% (the file's House rows short in part of the district; the state's sum −0.09% passes D3's part 1): the 13 local 2022 p have their own n; the 2024 local p of King and Pierce Counties take their lean from this file's House vote, a few points of lean at most, p within about 10%.
7. **North Dakota 2024** Governor −14.4%: the governor rows are absent in Burleigh and Divide Counties with the president whole; the governor's p is D2's, and no p of D3's rests on those rows by my reading (the legislative rows are present).
8. **New Jersey 2024** Bergen's municipality rows (item in §38.2 above): both rules drop them; no p moves (D3's part 3 says the same; the file stands −1.00% after the rule, NJ-12 −6.1% from blank lines).
9. **New Jersey 2018** NJ-06 −11.0%, NJ-12 −8.0% (Middlesex's file is ELECTION DAY only): #95 held Middlesex and Hunterdon's records; the moves are checked in 14.3.
10. **D2's governor totals** are the two leaders' sum where the source gives no more: Rhode Island 2018 +11.3%, West Virginia 2024 +6.9%, Wyoming 2022 +6.3%, Indiana 2024 +5.1% (Braun 1,566,081 against the state's 1,561,279, −0.3%), Missouri 2024 +2.5%, New Hampshire 2024 +2.3%, New Hampshire 2018 +1.5%, North Carolina 2020 +1.5%, Wyoming 2018 +0.5%; Connecticut 2018's Lamont 676,649 and New York 2018's Cuomo 3,424,416 are the Democratic line alone (the lines summed 694,509; 84% on the party line). 12. **Indiana 2024**: the state's files equal D1 and D2 (the governor +5.05% is D2's two-party total); against MEDSL's Indiana file the Senate is −7.8% and the House −5.3% (MEDSL's file is held back by D3); the 1,302 p rest on the state's files; no p moves.
13. **D1's and D2's records against MEDSL's state-level files** (§38.7): Senate 2006 WV −55%, 2008 GA −43%, 2014 LA +139%, 2010 UT −7.7%, 2010 IL +4.5%, 2018 MS −4.0%, 2016 LA +3.3%, 2018 PA −0.3%, 2018 WV −0.5%, 2022 AK +0.7%, 2006 ME +0.2%; the president 2004 MS −1.1%. ### 14.2 The comparison with D3's volume test (`c39_volume_compare.py`)

Read only after c38 stood: `data/local/check-volume.json` (parts 1–3) and REPORT §29. **Both lists**: 7 state-years (2016 ME, 2018 NJ, 2018 NY, 2022 AR, MI, NH, NY). **D3 alone**: 3 — 2018 IN (−0.70% by every House line summed; each contest within 0.5% by mine), 2022 IN (−57%) and 2022 NJ (+200%), where no p of D3's rests on the file. **c38 alone**: 119 — the coarse tier 28; a state's own file 12 (North Carolina's Board years, Indiana 2024); D1 or D2 against MEDSL's state-level file 12 (no p of D3's); Klarner's districts 31; MEDSL files' contests 36. The seven differences of method, each a reading (§39.4): (1) the unit — D3 sums a state's House lines, c38 reads each contest against its record (Washington 2022's 8th passes his, is listed by mine); (2) 2024 — D3's gate is the president alone, c38 reads every federal and statewide contest (North Dakota's governor rows, New York's Monroe and Ontario Counties); (3) fusion lines; (4) D2's two-party totals, which D3's part 1 matches by the leaders; (5) Klarner's districts — a doubling test (his) against every district off by 5% (mine); (6) no second count — 2020 untested (his), the odd-year legislatures, DC 2018, North Carolina's odd years and the coarse tier (mine); (7) subtotal rows, found by both by rules written apart (D3 drops a row equal to the rest of its county for every candidate; c38 a named total or a parent row equal to the rows that extend it).
